Nonparametric Inference on Dose-Response Curves Without the Positivity Condition
Yikun Zhang, Yen-Chi Chen, and Alexander Giessing

Abstract
Existing statistical methods in causal inference often assume the positivity condition, where every individual has some chance of receiving any treatment level regardless of covariates. This assumption could be violated in observational studies with continuous treatments. In this paper, we develop identification and estimation theories for causal effects with continuous treatments ( i.e. , dose-response curves) without relying on the positivity condition. Our approach identifies and estimates the derivative of the treatment effect for each observed sample, integrating it to the treatment level of interest to mitigate bias from the lack of positivity. The method is grounded in a weaker assumption, satisfied by additive confounding models. We propose a fast and reliable numerical recipe for computing our integral estimator in practice and derive its asymptotic properties. To enable valid inference on the dose-response curve and its derivative, we use the nonparametric bootstrap and establish its consistency. The performances of our proposed estimators are validated through simulation studies and an analysis of the effect of air pollution exposure (PM 2.5 ) on cardiovascular mortality rates. Keywords: Causal Inference; Dose-Response Curve; Positivity; Kernel Smoothing.



1
Introduction

In observational studies, causal effects do not always result from standard binary interventions but rather arise from continuous treatments or exposures. The causal effect of a continuous treatment variable
T\in\mathcal{T}\subset\mathbb{R}
on an outcome
Y\in\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathbb{R}
is described by the (causal) dose-response curve, which characterizes the average outcome if all individuals in the population were assigned to a treatment level
T=t
. Typically, an additional set of covariates
\bm{S}\in\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is observed, capturing potential confounding variables that influence both the treatment
T
and the outcome
Y
. Under appropriate regularity conditions, the dose-response curve coincides with the covariate-adjusted regression function
t\mapsto\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]
, where
\mu(t,\bm{s})=\mathbb{E}\left(Y|T=t,\bm{S}=\bm{s}\right)
, making it identifiable from the observed data for any
t\in\mathcal{T}

(
Robins et al., 2000
;
Neugebauer and van der Laan, 2007
;
Díaz and van der Laan, 2013
;
Kennedy et al., 2017
;
Takatsu and Westling, 2022
)
.

Figure 1
:
Simulation results under the single confounder model (
1
) with
n=2000
.
Left:
The support of the joint distribution of
(T,S)
.
Middle:
Estimated dose-response curves using naive regression adjustment (RA) and the proposed integral estimators, overlaid with the true one
m(t)
.
Right:
Estimated derivatives of the dose-response curve by naive RA and our proposed localized estimators, overlaid with the true derivative
\theta(t)
. The middle and right panels also present the 95% confidence intervals (CIs) and/or uniform confidence bands from our proposed estimators, shown as shaded regions and dashed lines, respectively.

However, the regularity conditions for identifying and estimating the dose-response curve may not always hold in observational studies. In particular, a key assumption in this context is the following positivity condition, which ensures sufficient variability in treatment assignment across all strata of covariates.

Assumption A0

(Positivity)
.

The conditional density
p(t|\bm{s})
is bounded above and away from zero almost surely for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
and
\bm{s}\in\mathcal{S}
.

The positivity condition (
A0
) may be violated in observational studies for two main reasons: (i) theoretically, it is impossible for some individuals with specific covariate values to receive certain treatment levels, and (ii) practically, a finite data sample may fail to collect individuals exposed to certain treatment levels; see
Cole and Hernán (2008)
;
Westreich and Cole (2010)
;
Petersen et al. (2012)
for related discussions. These issues are particularly pervasive in the context of continuous treatments. For instance, air pollution levels may vary significantly across larger regions while remaining relatively consistent within smaller areas, resulting in individuals in the same location being exposed to only one level of pollution. This discrepancy, where spatial covariates change at a finer scale than the exposure measurement, leads to violations of the positivity condition
(
Paciorek, 2010
;
Schnell and Papadogeorgou, 2020
;
Keller and Szpiro, 2020
)
. As an illustrative example, consider a single confounder model:

Y=T^{2}+T+1+10S+\epsilon,\quad T=\sin(\pi S)+E,\quad\text{ and }\quad S\sim\text{Uniform}[-1,1]\subset\mathbb{R},

(1)

where
E\sim\text{Uniform}[-0.3,0.3]
is an independent treatment variation and
\epsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
is an independent noise variable. Here, the marginal supports of
T
and
S
are
\mathcal{T}=[-1.3,1.3]
and
\mathcal{S}=[-1,1]
respectively, but the joint support of
(T,S)
covers only a narrow band within the product space
\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{S}
; see the left panel of
Figure 1
. In this scenario, the conditional density
p(t|s)
for any
s\in\mathcal{S}
is 0 in the gray regions, violating the positivity condition (
A0
). Without (
A0
), existing approaches for estimating the dose-response curve
t\mapsto m(t)
and its derivative
t\mapsto m^{\prime}(t):=\theta(t)
can be very unstable at certain treatment levels, as illustrated by the usual regression adjustment estimators (defined in (
9
) and Remark
1
below) in the middle and right panels of
Figure 1
.

To address the issues arising from violations of the positivity condition, we introduce a novel identification strategy for the dose-response curve
m(t)
and its derivative
\theta(t)
based on conditional expectations, thereby eliminating reliance on (
A0
). Leveraging this strategy, we propose an integral estimator of
m(t)
and a localized estimator of
\theta(t)
, both of which remain consistent even when the positivity condition (
A0
) fails in some regions of
\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{S}
; see the middle and right panels of
Figure 1
. Our key contributions and the paper outline are as follows:

1. Identification and Inference:
We illuminate identification conditions for
m(t)
and
\theta(t)
without requiring the positivity condition (
A0
) in
Section 2
. In
Section 3.1
, we introduce our integral estimator of
m(t)
, constructed by integrating a localized estimator of
\theta(t)
around the observed data, enabling extrapolation to any treatment level
t\in\mathcal{T}
of interest. Its practical utility is facilitated by Riemann sum approximations in
Section 3.2
and reliable inference through nonparametric bootstrap in
Section 3.3
.

2. Asymptotic Theory:
In
Section 4.2
, we establish the uniform consistency of our integral and localized derivative estimators within the kernel smoothing framework. Furthermore, the validity of nonparametric bootstrap inference is rigorously proved in
Section 4.3
.

3. Experiments:
We evaluate the finite-sample performances of our proposed estimators through simulations and a case study of the effects of fine particulate matter (PM
2.5
) on cardiovascular mortality rates in
Section 5
. Code for all our experiments is publicly available at
https://github.com/zhangyk8/npDoseResponse/tree/main/Paper_Code
, together with both Python package “
npDoseResponse
” and R package
(
Zhang and Chen, 2024
)
.

1.1
Other Related Works

Estimating the dose-response curve is technically challenging in causal inference because it is not pathwise differentiable and cannot be consistently estimated in a
\sqrt{n}
rate
(
Chamberlain, 1986
;
van der Vaart, 1991
)
. Parametrically,
Robins et al. (2000)
pioneered a marginal structural model to estimate
m(t)
, later extended by
van der Laan and Robins (2003)
;
Neugebauer and van der Laan (2007)
to relax dependence on parametric model specification through projection methods. Nonparametrically,
Newey (1994)
developed a regression adjustment approach using two-stage kernel smoothing, later adapted to dose-response curve estimation by
Flores (2007)
. However, the consistency and asymptotic normality of their kernel-based estimators require undersmoothing bandwidths, which complicates practical bandwidth selection; see Section 5.7 in
Wasserman (2006)
. On the contrary, our proposed integral estimator of
m(t)
, leveraging kernel-based derivative estimation, avoids the need for undersmoothing or explicit bias correction
(
Calonico et al., 2018
;
Takatsu and Westling, 2022
)
, allowing standard bandwidth selection methods in nonparametric regression.

There are many existing works about the estimation of average derivative effects in the literature; see
Härdle and Stoker (1989)
;
Powell et al. (1989)
;
Newey and Stoker (1993)
;
Cattaneo et al. (2010)
;
Hirshberg and Wager (2020)
and references therein. Under regularity conditions, average derivative effects are identical to the so-called incremental treatment effects, which are closely related to the derivatives
\theta(t)=m^{\prime}(t)
of dose-response curves; see Proposition 1 in
Rothenhäusler and Yu (2019)
and Section 6.1 in
Hines et al. (2023)
.

Most existing methods for estimating
m(t)
and
\theta(t)
assume the positivity condition (
A0
). For binary treatments, prior research has examined the impact of positivity violations on various estimators from the perspectives of convergence rates
(
Khan and Tamer, 2010
;
D’Amour et al., 2021
)
and empirical performances
(
Busso et al., 2014
;
Léger et al., 2022
)
. Approaches to address positivity violations include trimming extreme propensity score values
(
Dehejia and Wahba, 1999
;
Crump et al., 2009
;
Yang and Ding, 2018
;
Branson et al., 2023
)
and developing robust estimators
(
Rothe, 2017
;
Ma and Wang, 2020
)
. More recently,
Kennedy (2019)
;
Rothenhäusler and Yu (2019)
;
Schindl et al. (2024)
proposed incremental treatment effects as alternative estimands under positivity violations. To the best of our knowledge, no existing work provides nonparametric inference methods for
m(t)
and
\theta(t)
without assuming (
A0
).

1.2
Notations

Throughout the paper, we consider an outcome variable
Y\in\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathbb{R}
, univariate continuous treatment
T\in\mathcal{T}\subset\mathbb{R}
, and a vector of covariates
\bm{S}=(S_{1},...,S_{d})\in\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with a fixed dimension
d
. The data sample consists of independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) observations
\bm{U}_{i}=(Y_{i},T_{i},\bm{S}_{i}),i=1,...,n
with the common distribution
\mathrm{P}
and Lebesgue density
p(y,t,\bm{s})=p(y|t,\bm{s})\cdot p(t|\bm{s})\cdot p_{S}(\bm{s})
. Here,
p_{T}(t)
and
p_{S}(\bm{s})
are the marginal densities of
T
and
\bm{S}
, respectively, and
p(t|\bm{s})=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\left(T\leq t|\bm{S}=\bm{s}\right)
is the conditional density of
T
given covariates
\bm{S}=\bm{s}
. We also denote the joint density of
(T,\bm{S})
by
p(t,\bm{s})=p(t|\bm{s})\cdot p_{S}(\bm{s})=p(\bm{s}|t)\cdot p_{T}(t)
, whose support is
\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{S}
. Let
\mathbb{P}_{n}
be the empirical measure so that
\mathbb{P}_{n}g=\int g(\bm{u})\,d\mathbb{P}_{n}(\bm{u})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(\bm{U}_{i})
for some measurable function
g
. At the population level, we denote
\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}g=\mathbb{E}\left[g(\bm{U})\right]=\int g(\bm{u})\,d\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}(\bm{u})
for
\mathrm{P}
-integrable function
g
. In addition, we define the empirical process evaluated at a
\mathrm{P}
-integrable function
g
as
\mathbb{G}_{n}(g)=\sqrt{n}\left(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\right)g=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[g(\bm{U}_{i})-\mathbb{E}\left(g(\bm{U}_{i})\right)\right]
. Finally, we use
\mathbbm{1}_{A}
to denote the indicator function of a set
A
.

We use the big-
O
notation
h_{n}=O(g_{n})
if
|h_{n}|
is upper bounded by a positive constant multiple of
g_{n}>0
when
n
is sufficiently large. In contrast,
h_{n}=o(g_{n})
when
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{|h_{n}|}{g_{n}}=0
. For random variables, the notation
o_{P}(1)
is short for a sequence of random variables converging to zero in probability, while the expression
O_{P}(1)
denotes the sequence that is bounded in probability. We also use the notation
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
or
b_{n}\gtrsim a_{n}
when there exists an absolute constant
A>0
such that
a_{n}\leq Ab_{n}
when
n
is large. If
a_{n}\gtrsim b_{n}
and
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
, then
a_{n},b_{n}
are asymptotically equal and it is denoted by
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
.

2
Basic Setup and Identification

Following the potential outcome framework
(
Rubin, 1974
)
, we let
Y(t)
denote the potential outcome that would have been observed under treatment level
T=t
. The causal effect for the continuous treatment variable (
i.e.
, dose-response curve) can be written as
t\mapsto m(t)=\mathbb{E}\left[Y(t)\right]
, and its derivative effect curve is
t\mapsto\theta(t)=\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}\left[Y(t)\right]
. We first state some basic identification assumptions.

Assumption A1

(Basic identification conditions)
.

For any
t\in\mathcal{T}
, we have that

(a)

(Consistency)
T=t
implies that
Y(t)=Y
.

(b)

(Ignorability or unconfoundedness)
Y(t)
is conditionally independent of
T
given
\bm{S}
.

(c)

(Treatment variation) The conditional variance of
T
given any
\bm{S}=\bm{s}\in\mathcal{S}
is strictly positive,
i.e.
,
\mathrm{Var}(T|\bm{S}=\bm{s})>0
for any
\bm{s}\in\mathcal{S}
.

Assumption
A1
(a) is a continuous treatment version of the stable unit treatment value assumption (SUTVA; Page 19 of
Cox 1958
and
Rubin 1980
), ensuring no interference between units and no multiple versions of treatments. When both the treatment
T
and the outcome
Y
are continuous, the required consistency condition is essentially the equality of conditional distributions
\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\left(Y(T)\leq y|T=t\right)=\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\left(Y(t)\leq y|T=t\right)
for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
and
y\in\mathcal{Y}

(
Gill and Robins, 2001
)
. Assumption
A1
(b), the ignorability condition, was first generalized to continuous treatments by
Hirano and Imbens (2004)
and implies that the potential outcome variable is independent of the treatment within any specific strata of covariates. In spatial confounding, this condition holds when spatial locations are adjusted for
(
Gilbert et al., 2023
)
. It further ensures that the mean potential outcome under
T=t
is invariant across treatment levels, conditioned on
\bm{S}=\bm{s}
. Finally, Assumption
A1
(c), the treatment variation condition, is crucial for identifying the conditional mean outcome (or regression) function
\mu(t,\bm{s})=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left(Y|T=t,\bm{S}=\bm{s}\right)
over a non-degenerate region of
\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{S}
. We demonstrate how
\mathrm{Var}(T|\bm{S}=\bm{s})=0
for some
\bm{s}\in\mathcal{S}
can lead to ambiguous definitions of the associated dose-response curves in Example
1
of
Section A
. In cases where Assumption
A1
(c) fails, we also derive nonparametric bounds on
m(t)
and its derivative
\theta(t)
in
Section A
under an additive confounding model (
7
) stated below.

Unfortunately, Assumption
A1
alone is insufficient to identify the dose-response curve
m(t)=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[Y(t)\right]
with observable data in the standard manner, such as through the covariate-adjusted regression function
\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\mu(t,\bm{s})\cdot p_{S}(\bm{s})\,d\bm{s}
. The key reason is that without the positivity condition (
A0
), the conditional mean outcome function
\mu(t,\bm{s})=\mathbb{E}\left(Y|T=t,\bm{S}=\bm{s}\right)
is ill-defined in those regions of
\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{S}
outside the support
\mathcal{E}
of the joint density
p(t,\bm{s})
. To resolve this identification issue, we introduce an additional assumption to identify the derivative curve
\theta(t)=\frac{d}{dt}\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[Y(t)\right]
, subsequently linking the identification of
m(t)
to
\theta(t)
via an integral formula.

Assumption A2

(Extrapolation condition)
.

Suppose that at least one of the following assumptions are valid.

(a)

The function
\mathbb{E}\left[Y(t)|\bm{S}=\bm{s}\right]
is continuously differentiable with respect to
t
for any
(t,\bm{s})\in\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{S}
such that
p(\bm{s}|t)>0
, and the following equalities hold true:

\theta(t)=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathbb{E}\left[Y(t)|\bm{S}\right]\right]=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathbb{E}\left[Y(t)|\bm{S}\right]\Big|T=t\right].

(2)

(b)

The potential outcome
Y(t)
is continuously differentiable with respect to
t
, and the following equalities hold true:

\theta(t)=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}Y(t)\Big|\bm{S}\right]\right]=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}Y(t)\Big|\bm{S}\right]\Big|T=t\right].

(3)

Additionally, it holds true that
\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}(Y)=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[m(T)\right]
.

The first equalities of (
2
) and (
3
) essentially reflect the interchangeability of expectation and partial derivative operations, which hold under mild conditions. In particular, these equalities are valid if
\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathbb{E}\left[Y(t)|\bm{S}\right]\right|
or
\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}Y(t)\right|
are bounded by some integrable functions
\bar{\mu}_{1}(\bm{S}),\bar{\mu}_{2}(\bm{S})
with respect to the distribution of
\bm{S}
, respectively; see Theorem 1.1 and Example 1.8 in
Shao (2003)
. Additionally, the smoothness condition on
Y(t)
in Assumption
A2
(b) is not new, as it has been introduced in Assumption 3 of
Rothenhäusler and Yu (2019)
for identifying incremental effects. The key identification conditions in Assumption
A2
are captured by the second equalities of (
2
) and (
3
). As demonstrated by Proposition
1
, they bridge the connection between the hypothetical potential outcome regime and the observable data distribution.

Proposition 1

(Identification for
\theta(t)
)
.

Under Assumption
A1
, we have that

\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathbb{E}\left[Y(t)|\bm{S}\right]\Big|T=t\right]=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathbb{E}\left(Y|T=t,\bm{S}\right)\Big|T=t\right]:=\theta_{C}(t),

(4)

If, in addition,
\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}Y(t)\right|\Big|\bm{S}=\bm{s}\right]<\infty
for all
\bm{s}\in\mathcal{S}
, then

\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}Y(t)\Big|\bm{S}\right]\Big|T=t\right]=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathbb{E}\left[Y(t)|\bm{S}\right]\Big|T=t\right]=\theta_{C}(t).

(5)

Proof of Proposition
1
.

The equality (
4
) is a natural consequence of Assumption
A1
as:

\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathbb{E}\left[Y(t)|\bm{S}\right]\Big|T=t\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(*)}}}{{=}}\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathbb{E}\left[Y(t)|T=t,\bm{S}\right]\Big|T=t\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(**)}}}{{=}}\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathbb{E}\left[Y|T=t,\bm{S}\right]\Big|T=t\right],

where (*) follows from Assumption
A1
(b) and (**) is due to Assumption
A1
(a).

In addition, if
\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}Y(t)\right|\Big|\bm{S}=\bm{s}\right]<\infty
for all
\bm{s}\in\mathcal{S}
, then the interchangeability of partial derivative
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}
and conditional expectation
\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}(\cdot|\bm{S})
holds, and (
5
) follows.
∎

Under Assumption
A2
and Proposition
1
, we can identify the derivative curve
\theta(t)
through the form
\theta_{C}(t)=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})\Big|T=t\right]
, and we only require
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s})=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathbb{E}\left(Y|T=t,\bm{S}=\bm{s}\right)
to be well-defined whenever
p(\bm{s}|t)>0
for any
t\in\mathcal{T}
. This serves as our key technique to bypass the restrictive positivity condition (
A0
) in the context of continuous treatments.

Once
\theta(t)
is identifiable from observable data under Assumptions
A1
and
A2
, we can refer the identification and estimation back to the dose-response curve
m(t)
as follows. For any
t\in\mathcal{T}
, the fundamental theorem of calculus reveals that

m(t)=m(T)+\int_{\widetilde{t}=T}^{\widetilde{t}=t}m^{\prime}(\widetilde{t})\,d\widetilde{t}=m(T)+\int_{\widetilde{t}=T}^{\widetilde{t}=t}\theta(\widetilde{t})\,d\widetilde{t}.

Taking the expectation on both sides of the above equality under Assumption
A2
yields that

\displaystyle\begin{split}m(t)=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[m(T)+\int_{\widetilde{t}=T}^{\widetilde{t}=t}\theta(\widetilde{t})\,d\widetilde{t}\right]&=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}(Y)+\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left\{\int_{\widetilde{t}=T}^{\widetilde{t}=t}\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(\widetilde{t},\bm{S})\Big|T=\widetilde{t}\right]\,d\widetilde{t}\right\}.\end{split}

(6)

All the quantities on the right-hand of (
6
) are identifiable from the observed data
\left\{(Y_{i},T_{i},\bm{S}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
even without the positivity condition (
A0
); see
Section 3.1
for more details. To further demystify Assumption
A2
, we now present several structural equation modeling examples under which Assumption
A2
holds.

2.1
Motivating Example: Additive Confounding Model

The key running example in this paper is the additive confounding model defined as:

\displaystyle\begin{split}Y(t)&=\bar{m}(t)+\eta(\bm{S})+\epsilon,\end{split}

(7)

where
\bar{m}(t)
is the primary treatment effect of interest,
\eta(\bm{S})
is the random effect, and
\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}
is an independent noise variable with
\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}(\epsilon)=0
.

Under model (
7
) and Assumption
A1
(a), the outcome model becomes
Y=\bar{m}(T)+\eta(\bm{S})+\epsilon
. Such an additive form is a common working model in spatial confounding problems
(
Paciorek, 2010
;
Schnell and Papadogeorgou, 2020
)
and also known as the geoadditive structural equation model
(
Kammann and Wand, 2003
;
Thaden and Kneib, 2018
;
Wiecha and Reich, 2024
)
, where
\bm{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are the spatial locations (usually with
d=2
) or other spatially correlated covariates that affect both the treatment
T
and the outcome
Y
. We summarize some key properties of the additive confounding model (
7
) in the following proposition, whose proof is in
Section C.1
. In particular, Proposition
2
(c,d) verify Assumption
A2
under model (
7
).

Proposition 2

(Properties of the additive confounding model)
.

Let
\mu(t,\bm{s})=\mathbb{E}\left(Y|T=t,\bm{S}=\bm{s}\right)=\bar{m}(t)+\eta(\bm{s})
under the additive confounding model (
7
). Assume that
\bar{m}(t)
is differentiable for any
t\in\mathcal{T}
. The following results hold for any
t\in\mathcal{T}
under Assumption
A1
:

(a)

\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]=\bar{m}(t)
when
\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\eta(\bm{S})\right]=0
.

(b)

\theta(t)\neq\frac{d}{dt}\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})|T=t\right]
when
\frac{d}{dt}\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\eta(\bm{S})|T=t\right]\neq 0
.

(c)

\theta(t)=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})\big|T=t\right]=\theta_{C}(t)
.

(d)

\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}(Y)=\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(T,\bm{S})\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[m(T)\right]
, where
m(t)=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[Y(t)\right]
.

In addition to the additive confounding model (
7
), Assumption
A2
holds under the following more general model:

Y(t)=\bar{m}(t)+\eta(\bm{S}_{1},\bm{S}_{2})+q(\bm{S}_{1},t)\cdot\Phi(\bm{S}_{2})+\epsilon,

(8)

where the covariate vector is
\bm{S}=(\bm{S}_{1},\bm{S}_{2})^{T}\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathcal{S}_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\eta:\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathcal{S}_{2}\to\mathbb{R},q:\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathcal{T}\to\mathbb{R},\Phi:\mathcal{S}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
are deterministic functions. Furthermore, the random variable
\Phi(\bm{S}_{2})
needs to satisfy that

\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\Phi(\bm{S}_{2})\big|\bm{S}_{1}=\bm{s}_{1},T=t\right]=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\Phi(\bm{S}_{2})\big|\bm{S}_{1}=\bm{s}_{1}\right]=0

for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
and
\bm{s}_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
.

3
Nonparametric Inference Without the Positivity Condition

Under the positivity condition (
A0
) and Assumption
A1
, the dose-response curve
m(t)
can be identified through the covariate-adjusted regression function
\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]
from the observed data
\left\{(Y_{i},T_{i},\bm{S}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
, suggesting the following regression adjustment (RA) or G-computation estimator
(
Robins, 1986
;
Gill and Robins, 2001
)
:

\widehat{m}_{RA}(t)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{i}),

(9)

where
\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{s})
is any consistent estimator of the conditional mean outcome function
\mu(t,\bm{s})=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left(Y|T=t,\bm{S}=\bm{s}\right)
. As discussed in
Section 2
, the above estimator (
9
) becomes invalid and inconsistent for estimating
m(t)
when the positivity condition (
A0
) fails in some region of
\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{S}
.
Similarly, other existing methods for estimating
m(t)
, such as the inverse probability weighted (IPW) or its augmented variants, also rely on (
A0
) for their consistency and empirical performances
(
Díaz and van der Laan, 2013
;
Kennedy et al., 2017
;
Kallus and Zhou, 2018
;
Colangelo and Lee, 2020
)
. The same issue incurred by the failure of (
A0
) applies to the estimation of
\theta(t)=m^{\prime}(t)
.

In this section, utilizing our identification assumption (
A2
) and the integral formula (
6
), we propose a novel integral estimator to address the inconsistency of RA estimators for
m(t)
and
\theta(t)
in the absence of (
A0
).
Furthermore, we outline a fast algorithm for computing our integral estimator of
m(t)
in practice and delineate bootstrap inference procedures for both estimators of
\theta(t)
and
m(t)
.

3.1
Proposed Integral Estimator of
m(t)

Given the observed data
\left\{(Y_{i},T_{i},\bm{S}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
, our estimation strategy for addressing violations the positivity condition (
A0
) relies on three critical insights.

•

Insight 1: Estimation of
\mu(t,\bm{s})
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s})
.
Since the observed data
(T_{i},\bm{S}_{i}),i=1,...,n
typically fall within high density regions of
p(t,\bm{s})
, the conditional mean outcome function
\mu(t,\bm{s})
is well-defined and estimable at each observation
(T_{i},\bm{S}_{i})
, enabling consistent estimation of the partial derivative
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s})
at
(T_{i},\bm{S}_{i})
as well.

•

Insight 2: Estimation of
\theta(t)
via
\theta_{C}(t)
.

The localized form
\theta_{C}(t)=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})\Big|T=t\right]
only requires accurate estimation of
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s})
at those covariate vectors
\bm{s}
with high conditional density
p(\bm{s}|t)
.

•

Insight 3: Integral Relation Between
\theta(t)
and
m(t)
.

The integral formula (
6
) suggests that as long as we have consistent estimators of
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s})
at each
(T_{i},\bm{S}_{i})
and
\theta(t)
near
T_{i}
, we can extrapolate the estimation to
m(t)
for any
t\in\mathcal{T}
via integration even when
p(t,\bm{s})=0
for some
\bm{s}\in\mathcal{S}
.

Building on these insights, we propose an
integral estimator
of the dose-response curve
m(t)
as:

\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[Y_{i}+\int_{\widetilde{t}=T_{i}}^{\widetilde{t}=t}\widehat{\theta}_{C}(\widetilde{t})\,d\widetilde{t}\right],

(10)

where
\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)
is a consistent estimator of
\theta_{C}(t)=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})\Big|T=t\right]=\int\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s})\,d\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}(\bm{s}|t)
. The construction of
\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)
requires estimation of two nuisance functions:

(i)

the partial derivative
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s})
of the conditional mean outcome function
\mu(t,\bm{s})
;

(ii)

the conditional cumulative distribution function (CDF)
P(\bm{s}|t)
.

In this paper, we employ the following kernel smoothing methods for estimating these two nuisance functions and leave other possibilities for future exploration.

3.1.1
Local Polynomial Regression Estimator of
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\mathbf{s})

We consider estimating
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s})
by local polynomial regression
(
Fan and Gijbels, 1996
)
due to its robustness around the boundary of support, known as the automatic kernel carpentry
(
Hastie and Loader, 1993
)
. Let
K_{T}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty),\,K_{S}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
be two symmetric kernel functions, and let
h,b>0
be two smoothing bandwidth parameters. Some common univariate kernel functions include the Epanechnikov kernel
K(u)=\frac{3}{4}\left(1-u^{2}\right)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\{|u|\leq 1\}}
and Gaussian kernel
K(u)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{2}\right)
. For a multivariate kernel function, we use the product kernel technique as
K_{S}(\bm{u})=\prod_{i=1}^{d}K(u_{i})
for
\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

To estimate
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s})
from the observed data
\{(Y_{i},T_{i},\bm{S}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, we fit a partial local polynomial regression of order
q\geq 1
, with monomials in
\{(T_{i}-t)\}_{i=1}^{n}
as the polynomial basis for the treatment variable
T
and a local linear function for the covariate vector
\bm{S}

(
Ruppert and Wand, 1994
)
. Specifically, we define
\bm{X}_{i}(t,\bm{s})=\left(1,(T_{i}-t),...,(T_{i}-t)^{q},(S_{i,1}-s_{1}),...,(S_{i,d}-s_{d})\right)^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{q+1+d}
for
i=1,...,n
and consider

\displaystyle\left(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}(t,\bm{s}),\widehat{\bm{\alpha}}(t,\bm{s})\right)^{T}

(11)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{(\bm{\beta},\bm{\alpha})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{q+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[Y_{i}-\sum_{j=0}^{q}\beta_{j}(T_{i}-t)^{q}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\alpha_{\ell}(S_{i,\ell}-s_{\ell})\right]^{2}K_{T}\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)K_{S}\left(\frac{\bm{S}_{i}-\bm{s}}{b}\right)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{(\bm{\beta},\bm{\alpha})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{q+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(Y_{i}-\bm{X}_{i}(t,\bm{s})^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\bm{\beta}\\
\bm{\alpha}\end{pmatrix}\right)^{2}K_{T}\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)K_{S}\left(\frac{\bm{S}_{i}-\bm{s}}{b}\right).

For simplicity, we use the same bandwidth parameter
b
for each coordinate in
K_{S}
. One can generalize the above method and related theoretical results to a general bandwidth matrix for
K_{S}
with little effort. Let
\bm{X}(t,\bm{s})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times(q+1+d)}
be a matrix with the
j
-th row as
\bm{X}_{j}(t,\bm{s})^{T}
and
\bm{Y}=(Y_{1},...,Y_{n})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We define a diagonal weight matrix as:

\bm{W}(t,\bm{s})=\mathtt{Diag}\left(K_{T}\left(\frac{T_{1}-t}{h}\right)K_{S}\left(\frac{\bm{S}_{1}-\bm{s}}{b}\right),...,K_{T}\left(\frac{T_{n}-t}{h}\right)K_{S}\left(\frac{\bm{S}_{n}-\bm{s}}{b}\right)\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}.

Thus, (
11
) is a weighted least square problem and has a closed-form solution as:

\left(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}(t,\bm{s}),\widehat{\bm{\alpha}}(t,\bm{s})\right)^{T}=\left[\bm{X}^{T}(t,\bm{s})\bm{W}(t,\bm{s})\bm{X}(t,\bm{s})\right]^{-1}\bm{X}(t,\bm{s})^{T}\bm{W}(t,\bm{s})\bm{Y}.

(12)

The second component
\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})
of the fitted coefficient
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}(t,\bm{s})
provides a natural estimator of
\beta_{2}(t,\bm{s}):=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s})
. In practice, we recommend choosing
q
to be an even number in (
11
), because there is an increment to the asymptotic variance of
\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})
when
q
changes from an even number to the consecutive odd number. Additionally, fitting (partial) local polynomial regressions of higher orders often give rise to a possible reduction of bias but also a substantial increase of the variability; see Chapter 3.3 in
Fan and Gijbels (1996)
. We will mainly focus on (partial) local quadratic regression with
q=2
when constructing our derivative estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})
in the subsequent analysis.

3.1.2
Kernel-Based Conditional CDF Estimator of
\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}(\mathbf{s}|t)

We consider estimating
\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}(\bm{s}|t)
through a Nadaraya-Watson conditional CDF estimator
(
Hall et al., 1999
)
defined as:

\widehat{P}_{\hslash}(\bm{s}|t)=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\bm{S}_{i}\leq\bm{s}\}}\cdot\bar{K}_{T}\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{\hslash}\right)}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bar{K}_{T}\left(\frac{T_{j}-t}{\hslash}\right)},

(13)

where
\bar{K}_{T}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
is again a kernel function and
\hslash>0
is a smoothing bandwidth parameter that needs not be the same as the bandwidth
h>0
used in estimating
\beta_{2}(t,\bm{s})=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s})
via local polynomial regression. Practically, several strategies for selecting
\hslash>0
in (
13
) are discussed by
Bashtannyk and Hyndman (2001)
;
Holmes et al. (2012)
.

Combining the partial derivative estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})
from (
12
) with the conditional CDF estimator
\widehat{P}_{\hslash}(\bm{s}|t)
from (
13
), we obtain the final localized estimator of
\theta_{C}(t)=\int\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s})\,d\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}(\bm{s}|t)
as:

\displaystyle\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)

\displaystyle=\int\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})\,d\widehat{P}_{\hslash}(\bm{s}|t)=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{S}_{i})\cdot\bar{K}_{T}\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{\hslash}\right)}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bar{K}_{T}\left(\frac{T_{j}-t}{\hslash}\right)}.

(14)

In essence,
\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)
is a regression adjustment (RA) estimator with two nuisance functions:
\beta_{2}(t,\bm{s})=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s})
and
P(\bm{s}|t)
. We have showcased how to use the (partial) local polynomial regression (
11
) to estimate
\beta_{2}(t,\bm{s})
and Nadaraya-Watson conditional CDF estimator (
13
) to estimate
P(\bm{s}|t)
.

Remark 1

(RA estimator of
\theta(t)
)
.

Under the additive confounding model (
7
), one can directly estimate
\theta(t)
via
\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})
or the RA estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{RA}(t)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{S}_{i})
, because, as shown in Proposition
2
,

\theta(t)=\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{m}(t)+\eta(\bm{S})\right]=\bar{m}^{\prime}(t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s})=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})\right].

However, the estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})
is suboptimal, as it only uses information from a single location.
Furthermore,
\widehat{\theta}_{RA}(t)
may not be stable and consistent when the positivity condition fails at certain
(t,\bm{S}_{i})\in\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{S}
; see
Figure 1
for an illustration. In contrast, our proposed estimator in (
14
), which relies on the conditional CDF estimator, remains valid and consistent even when the positivity condition (
A0
) does not hold.

Remark 2

(Linear smoother)
.

It is worth noting that our integral estimator (
10
) under the kernel-based estimator (
14
) is indeed a linear smoother. Let
\bm{e}_{2}=(0,1,0,...,0)^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{q+1+d}
. From (
12
) and
\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})=\bm{e}_{2}^{T}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}(t,\bm{s})
, we can express
\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)
in (
14
) as:

\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)=\left\{\int\bm{e}_{2}^{T}\left[\bm{X}^{T}(t,\bm{s})\bm{W}(t,\bm{s})\bm{X}(t,\bm{s})\right]^{-1}\bm{X}(t,\bm{s})^{T}\bm{W}(t,\bm{s})\,d\widehat{P}_{\hslash}(\bm{s}|t)\right\}\bm{Y}.

This implies that

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}+\left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\widetilde{t}=T_{i}}^{\widetilde{t}=t}\int\bm{e}_{2}^{T}\left[\bm{X}^{T}(t,\bm{s})\bm{W}(t,\bm{s})\bm{X}(t,\bm{s})\right]^{-1}\bm{X}(t,\bm{s})^{T}\bm{W}(t,\bm{s})\,d\widehat{P}_{\hslash}(\bm{s}|t)\,d\widetilde{t}\right\}\bm{Y}

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}l_{i}(t)Y_{i}.

As a consequence, we can apply the theory of linear smoothers to derive the effective degrees of freedom and fine-tune the smoothing bandwidth parameters
(
Buja et al., 1989
;
Wasserman, 2006
)
.

3.2
Fast Computing Algorithm for the Proposed Integral Estimator

Our proposed estimator
\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[Y_{i}+\int_{T_{i}}^{t}\widehat{\theta}_{C}(\widetilde{t})\,d\widetilde{t}\right]
of the dose-response curve
m(t)
involves an integral that can be analytically difficult to compute in practice. Therefore, we propose an efficient numerical approximation method to evaluate
\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)
with an error of order at most
O_{P}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)
. The key idea is to approximate the integral via Riemann sum, evaluate
\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)
only at the observed data points
T_{1},...,T_{n}
, and use linear interpolation to estimate the value at any arbitrary point
t\in\mathcal{T}
.

Let
T_{(1)}\leq\cdots\leq T_{(n)}
be the order statistics of
T_{1},...,T_{n}
and
\Delta_{j}=T_{(j+1)}-T_{(j)}
for
j=1,...,n-1
be the consecutive differences. The integral estimator
\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)
in (
10
) can be rewritten as:

\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\widetilde{t}=T_{(i)}}^{\widetilde{t}=t}\widehat{\theta}_{C}(\widetilde{t})\,d\widetilde{t}.

To compute
\widehat{m}_{\theta}(T_{(j)})
at the
j
-th order statistic, we approximate the integral term
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{T_{(i)}}^{T_{(j)}}\widehat{\theta}_{C}(\widetilde{t})\,d\widetilde{t}
above as follows.

•

When
i<j
, the integral is approximated by
\int_{\widetilde{t}=T_{(i)}}^{\widetilde{t}=T_{(j)}}\widehat{\theta}_{C}(\widetilde{t})\,d\widetilde{t}\approx\sum_{\ell=i}^{j-1}\widehat{\theta}_{C}(T_{(\ell)})\Delta_{\ell}
.

•

When
i>j
, the integral is approximated by
\int_{\widetilde{t}=T_{(i)}}^{\widetilde{t}=T_{(j)}}\widehat{\theta}_{C}(\widetilde{t})\,d\widetilde{t}\approx-\sum_{\ell=j}^{i-1}\widehat{\theta}_{C}(T_{(\ell+1)})\Delta_{\ell}
.

Substituting the above results into
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{T_{(i)}}^{T_{(j)}}\widehat{\theta}_{C}(\widetilde{t})\,d\widetilde{t}
for
1<j<n
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\widetilde{t}=T_{(i)}}^{\widetilde{t}=T_{(j)}}\widehat{\theta}_{C}(\widetilde{t})\,d\widetilde{t}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{n}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\sum_{\ell=i}^{j-1}\widehat{\theta}_{C}(T_{(\ell)})\Delta_{\ell}-\sum_{i=j}^{n}\sum_{\ell=j}^{i-1}\widehat{\theta}_{C}(T_{(\ell+1)})\Delta_{\ell}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{n}\left[\sum_{\ell=1}^{j-1}\ell\cdot\widehat{\theta}_{C}(T_{(\ell)})\Delta_{\ell}-\sum_{\ell=j}^{n-1}(n-\ell)\cdot\widehat{\theta}_{C}(T_{(\ell+1)})\Delta_{\ell}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\Delta_{i}\left[i\cdot\widehat{\theta}_{C}(T_{(i)})\mathbbm{1}_{\{i<j\}}-(n-i)\cdot\widehat{\theta}_{C}(T_{(i+1)})\mathbbm{1}_{\{i\geq j\}}\right],

where the equality (i) follows from switching the orders of summations. Similarly, when
j=1
or
j=n
, we also have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\widetilde{t}=T_{(i)}}^{\widetilde{t}=T_{(j)}}\widehat{\theta}_{C}(\widetilde{t})\,d\widetilde{t}

\displaystyle\approx\begin{cases}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=2}^{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{i-1}\widehat{\theta}_{C}(T_{(\ell+1)})\Delta_{\ell}&\text{ when }j=1,\\
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\sum_{\ell=i}^{n-1}\widehat{\theta}_{C}(T_{(\ell)})\Delta_{\ell}&\text{ when }j=n,\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}(n-\ell)\cdot\widehat{\theta}_{C}(T_{(\ell+1)})\Delta_{\ell}&\text{ when }j=1,\\
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}\ell\cdot\widehat{\theta}_{C}(T_{(\ell)})\Delta_{\ell}&\text{ when }j=n,\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\Delta_{i}\left[i\cdot\widehat{\theta}_{C}(T_{(i)})\mathbbm{1}_{\{i<j\}}-(n-i)\cdot\widehat{\theta}_{C}(T_{(i+1)})\mathbbm{1}_{\{i\geq j\}}\right].

Thus, the final approximation for
\widehat{m}_{\theta}(T_{(j)})
is given by:

\widehat{m}_{\theta}(T_{(j)})\approx\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\Delta_{i}\left[i\cdot\widehat{\theta}_{C}(T_{(i)})\mathbbm{1}_{\{i<j\}}-(n-i)\cdot\widehat{\theta}_{C}(T_{(i+1)})\mathbbm{1}_{\{i\geq j\}}\right].

(15)

To evaluate
\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)
at any arbitrary point
t
, we use linear interpolation between
\widehat{m}_{\theta}(T_{(j)})
and
\widehat{m}_{\theta}(T_{(j+1)})
over the interval
t\in\left[T_{(j)},T_{(j+1)}\right]
. The main advantage of using the approximation formula (
15
) is that it requires computing the derivative estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)
only at the order statistics
T_{(1)},...,T_{(n)}
.
When
\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)
and its derivative
\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)
are Lipschitz continuous and the marginal density
p_{T}(t)
is uniformly bounded away from 0 within the region of interest, the error of this approximation formula is at most
O_{P}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)
. This approximation error is asymptotically negligible compared to the dominating estimation error of
\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)
itself; see
Theorem 4
for further details.

3.3
Bootstrap Inference

Since deriving consistent estimators of the (asymptotic) variances of our integral estimator (
10
) and localized derivative estimator (
14
) is complicated, we propose conducting inference on
m(t)
and
\theta(t)
through the empirical bootstrap method
(
Efron, 1979
)
as follows.

1.

Compute the integral estimator
\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)
and localized derivative estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)
on the original data
\{(Y_{i},T_{i},\bm{S}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
.

2.

Generate
B
bootstrap samples
\left\{\left(Y_{i}^{*(b)},T_{i}^{*(b)},\bm{S}_{i}^{*(b)}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{n},b=1,...,B
by sampling with replacement from the original data and compute the integral estimator
\widehat{m}_{\theta}^{*(b)}(t)
and localized derivative estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{C}^{*(b)}(t)
on each bootstrapped sample for
b=1,...,B
.

3.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
be a pre-specified significance level.

•

For a pointwise inference at
t_{0}\in\mathcal{T}
, calculate the
1-\alpha
quantiles
\zeta_{1-\alpha}^{*}(t_{0})
and
\bar{\zeta}_{1-\alpha}^{*}(t_{0})
of
\{D_{1}(t_{0}),...,D_{B}(t_{0})\}
and
\{\bar{D}_{1}(t_{0}),...,\bar{D}_{B}(t_{0})\}
respectively, where
D_{b}(t_{0})=\left|\widehat{m}_{\theta}^{*(b)}(t_{0})-\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t_{0})\right|
and
\bar{D}_{b}(t_{0})=\left|\widehat{\theta}_{C}^{*(b)}(t_{0})-\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t_{0})\right|
for
b=1,...,B
.

•

For an uniform inference on the entire dose-response curve
m(t)
and its derivative
\theta(t)
, compute the
1-\alpha
quantiles
\xi_{1-\alpha}^{*}
and
\bar{\xi}_{1-\alpha}^{*}
of
\{D_{\sup,1},...,D_{\sup,B}\}
and
\{\bar{D}_{\sup,1},...,\bar{D}_{\sup,B}\}
respectively, where
D_{\sup,b}=\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\left|\widehat{m}_{\theta}^{*(b)}(t)-\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)\right|
and
\bar{D}_{\sup,b}=\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\left|\widehat{\theta}_{C}^{*(b)}(t)-\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)\right|
for
b=1,...,B
.

4.

Define the
1-\alpha
confidence intervals for
m(t_{0})
and
\theta(t_{0})
as:

\left[\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t_{0})-\zeta_{1-\alpha}^{*}(t_{0}),\,\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t_{0})+\zeta_{1-\alpha}^{*}(t_{0})\right]\;\text{ and }\;\left[\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t_{0})-\bar{\zeta}_{1-\alpha}^{*}(t_{0}),\,\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t_{0})+\bar{\zeta}_{1-\alpha}^{*}(t_{0})\right],

respectively, as well as the simultaneous
1-\alpha
confidence bands as:

\left[\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)-\xi_{1-\alpha}^{*},\,\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)+\xi_{1-\alpha}^{*}\right]\quad\text{ and }\quad\left[\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)-\bar{\xi}_{1-\alpha}^{*},\,\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)+\bar{\xi}_{1-\alpha}^{*}\right]

for every
t\in\mathcal{T}
.

In
Section 4.3
, we will establish the consistency of the above bootstrap inference procedures.

4
Asymptotic Theory

In this section, we study the consistency results of our integral estimator (
10
) and localized derivative estimator (
14
), along with the validity of bootstrap inference described in
Section 3.3
.

4.1
Notations and Assumptions

We introduce the regularity conditions for our subsequent theoretical analysis. Recall that
\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{S}
is the support of the joint density
p(t,\bm{s})
,
\mathcal{E}^{\circ}
is the interior of
\mathcal{E}
, and
\partial\mathcal{E}
is the boundary of
\mathcal{E}
.

Assumption A3

(Differentiability of the conditional mean outcome function)
.

(a)

For any
(t,\bm{s})\in\mathcal{E}^{\circ}
, the conditional mean outcome function
\mu(t,\bm{s})
is at least
(q+1)
times continuously differentiable with respect to
t
and at least four times continuously differentiable with respect to
\bm{s}
, where
q
is the order of (partial) local polynomial regression in (
11
).

(b)

All these partial derivatives of
\mu(t,\bm{s})
are continuous up to the boundary
\partial\mathcal{E}
.

(c)

\mu(t,\bm{s})
and the partial derivatives are uniformly bounded on
\mathcal{E}
.

(d)

There exist absolute constants
\sigma,A_{0}>0
such that
\mathrm{Var}(Y|T=t,\bm{S}=\bm{s})=\sigma^{2}
and
\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}|Y|^{4}<A_{0}<\infty
uniformly in
\mathcal{E}
.

Assumption A4

(Differentiability of the joint density)
.

(a)

The joint density
p(t,\bm{s})
is bounded and at least twice continuously differentiable with bounded partial derivatives up to the second order on
\mathcal{E}^{\circ}
.

(b)

All these partial derivatives of
p(t,\bm{s})
are continuous up to the boundary
\partial\mathcal{E}
.

(c)

\mathcal{E}
is compact and
p(t,\bm{s})
is uniformly bounded away from 0 on
\mathcal{E}
.

(d)

The marginal density
p_{T}(t)
of
T
is non-degenerate,
i.e.
, its support
\mathcal{T}
has a nonempty interior.

Assumptions
A3
and
A4
are standard differentiability conditions in local polynomial regression literature
(
Ruppert and Wand, 1994
;
Fan and Gijbels, 1996
)
, which can be slightly relaxed by the Hölder continuity condition. These conditions ensure that the bias term of
\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})
from the local polynomial regression (
12
) converges to 0 of the standard order
O(h^{q})+O(b^{2})
; see Lemma
3
below. Notice that the homogeneous error condition in Assumption
A3
(d) is imposed only for simplifying the proof. Our asymptotic theories hold for heterogeneous errors that are uniformly bounded away from 0 in
\mathcal{E}
. Additionally, the projection
\mathrm{proj}_{T}(\mathcal{E})
of the joint density support
\mathcal{E}
onto the domain of
T
coincides with the marginal support
\mathcal{T}
. Hence,
\mathcal{T}
is compact under Assumption
A4
.

To mitigate the boundary effects of the local polynomial regression, we impose the following conditions on
p(t,\bm{s})
,
\mu(t,\bm{s})
, and the geometric structure of
\mathcal{E}
near its boundary
\partial\mathcal{E}
.

Assumption A5

(Boundary conditions)
.

(a)

There exists some constants
r_{1},r_{2}\in(0,1)
such that for any
(t,\bm{s})\in\mathcal{E}
and all
\delta\in(0,r_{1}]
, there is a point
(t^{\prime},\bm{s}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{E}
satisfying

\mathcal{B}\left((t^{\prime},\bm{s}^{\prime}),\,r_{2}\delta\right)\subset\mathcal{B}\left((t,\bm{s}),\,\delta\right)\cap\mathcal{E},

where
\mathcal{B}((t,\bm{s}),\,r)=\left\{(t_{1},\bm{s}_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}:\left|\left|(t_{1}-t,\bm{s}_{1}-\bm{s})\right|\right|_{2}\leq r\right\}
with
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{2}
being the standard Euclidean norm.

(b)

For any
(t,\bm{s})\in\partial\mathcal{E}
, the boundary of
\mathcal{E}
, it satisfies that
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p(t,\bm{s})=\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{j}}p(t,\bm{s})=0
and
\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial s_{j}^{2}}\mu(t,\bm{s})=0
for all
j=1,...,d
.

(c)

For any
\delta>0
, the Lebesgue measure of the set
\partial\mathcal{E}\oplus\delta
satisfies
\left|\partial\mathcal{E}\oplus\delta\right|\leq A_{1}\cdot\delta
for some absolute constant
A_{1}>0
, where
\partial\mathcal{E}\oplus\delta=\left\{\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}:\inf_{\bm{x}\in\partial\mathcal{E}}\left|\left|\bm{z}-\bm{x}\right|\right|_{2}\leq\delta\right\}
.

Assumption
A5
(a) is adopted from the boundary condition (Assumption X) in
Fan and Guerre (2015)
, whose primitive and stronger version also appeared as Assumption (A4) in
Ruppert and Wand (1994)
. This is a relatively mild condition that holds when the boundary
\partial\mathcal{E}
is smooth or contains non-smooth vertices from regular structures, such as
d+1
dimensional cubes or convex cones. Indeed, Assumption
A5
(a) remains valid if any ball inside
\mathcal{E}
that centers near a vertex of
\partial\mathcal{E}
has its radius shrunk linearly when approaching the vertex. More examples and discussions about Assumption
A5
(a) are available in
Ruppert and Wand (1994)
;
Fan and Guerre (2015)
. The main purpose of this support condition is to ensure sufficient observations near the boundary so that the rate of convergence for our local polynomial estimator
\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})
at boundary points matches that at interior points of
\mathcal{E}
. Assumption
A5
(b) also regularizes the slope of
p(t,\bm{s})
and the curvature of
\mu(t,\bm{s})
at any boundary point
(t,\bm{s})\in\partial\mathcal{E}
, requiring
p(t,\bm{s})
to be flat and
\mu(t,\bm{s})
to exhibit zero curvature at
\partial\mathcal{E}
. Similar to Assumption
A4
, the partial derivatives
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p(t,\bm{s}),\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{j}}p(t,\bm{s})
are computed as limits from nearby interior points. This condition is also essential for maintaining the rate of convergence for the bias term of
\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})
at boundary points. Finally, Assumption
A5
(c) ensures that
\partial\mathcal{E}
does not exhibit fractal-like or irregular structures that lead to an infinite perimeter.

To establish the (uniform) consistency of our local polynomial regression estimator (
12
) and Nadaraya-Watson conditional CDF estimator (
13
), we impose the following regularity conditions on the kernel functions.

Assumption A6

(Regular kernel and VC-type conditions)
.

(a)

The functions
K_{T}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
and
K_{S}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
are compactly supported and Lispchitz continuous kernels such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{T}(t)\,dt=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K_{S}(\bm{s})\,d\bm{s}=1
,
K_{T}(t)=K_{T}(-t)
, and
K_{S}
is radially symmetric with
\int\bm{s}\cdot K_{S}(\bm{s})d\bm{s}=\bm{0}
. In addition, for all
j=1,2,...,
and
\ell=1,...,d
, it holds that

\displaystyle\kappa_{j}^{(T)}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{j}K_{T}(u)\,du<\infty,\quad\nu_{j}^{(T)}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{j}K_{T}^{2}(u)\,du<\infty,

\displaystyle\kappa_{j,\ell}^{(S)}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u_{\ell}^{j}K_{S}(\bm{u})\,d\bm{u}<\infty,\quad\text{ and }\quad\nu_{j,k}^{(S)}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u_{\ell}^{j}K_{S}^{2}(\bm{u})\,d\bm{u}<\infty.

Finally, both
K_{T}
and
K_{S}
are second-order kernels,
i.e.
,
\kappa_{2}^{(T)}>0
and
\kappa_{2,\ell}^{(S)}>0
for all
\ell=1,...,d
.

(b)

Let
\mathcal{K}_{q,d}=\Big\{(y,\bm{z})\mapsto\left(\frac{y-t}{h}\right)^{\ell}\left(\frac{z_{i}-s_{i}}{b}\right)^{k_{1}}\left(\frac{z_{j}-s_{j}}{b}\right)^{k_{2}}K_{T}\left(\frac{y-t}{h}\right)K_{S}\left(\frac{\bm{z}-\bm{s}}{b}\right):(t,\bm{s})\in\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{S};i,j=1,...,d;\ell=0,...,2q;k_{1},k_{2}=0,1;h,b>0\Big\}
. It holds that
\mathcal{K}_{q,d}
is a bounded VC-type class of measurable functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
.

(c)

The function
\bar{K}_{T}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
is a second-order, Lipschitz continuous, and symmetric kernel with a compact support,
i.e.
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bar{K}_{T}(t)\,dt=1
,
\bar{K}_{T}(t)=\bar{K}_{T}(-t)
, and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}t^{2}\bar{K}_{T}(t)\,dt\in(0,\infty)
.

(d)

Let
\bar{\mathcal{K}}=\left\{y\mapsto\bar{K}_{T}\left(\frac{y-t}{\hslash}\right):t\in\mathcal{T},\hslash>0\right\}
. It holds that
\bar{\mathcal{K}}
is a bounded VC-type class of measurable functions on
\mathbb{R}
.

Assumption
A6
(a,c) describe inherent properties of commonly used kernel functions rather than being regularity conditions. Assumption
A6
(b,d) are critical for the uniform consistency of kernel-based estimators
(
Giné and Guillou, 2002
;
Einmahl and Mason, 2005
)
and can be satisfied by many kernel functions, such as Gaussian and Epanechnikov kernels. For example, these assumptions hold when the kernel is a composite function of a polynomial in
d\geq 1
variables and a real-valued function of bounded variation; see Lemma 22 in
Nolan and Pollard (1987)
. Since most of the kernel functions are bounded, we can always take constant envelope functions for
\mathcal{K}_{q,d}
and
\bar{\mathcal{K}}
in Assumption
A6
(b,d).

4.2
Consistency of the Integral Estimator

Before establishing the consistency of our proposed integral estimator (
10
) and localized derivative estimator (
14
), we first study the uniform consistency of
\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})
from the local polynomial regression (
12
) as a building block. The pointwise consistency of
\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})
is detailed in
Section B
instead.

Lemma 3

(Uniform convergence of
\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})
)
.

Let
q>0
. Suppose that Assumptions
A3
,
A4
,
A5
, and
A6
(a,b) hold. Let
\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})
be the second element of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}(t,\bm{s})\in\mathbb{R}^{q+1}
and
\beta_{2}(t,\bm{s})=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s})
. Then, as
h,b,\frac{\max\{h,b\}^{4}}{h}\to 0
and
\frac{nh^{3}b^{d}}{|\log(hb^{d})|},\frac{|\log(hb^{d})|}{\log\log n}\to\infty
, we have that

\sup_{(t,\bm{s})\in\mathcal{E}}\left|\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})-\beta_{2}(t,\bm{s})\right|=O\left(h^{q}+b^{2}+\frac{\max\{b,h\}^{4}}{h}\right)+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{|\log(hb^{d})|}{nh^{3}b^{d}}}\right).

The proof of Lemma
3
is in
Section C.2
. At a high level, our rates of convergence for
\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})
in Lemma
3
are consistent with standard results in local polynomial regression literature
(
Ruppert and Wand, 1994
;
Fan and Gijbels, 1996
;
Lu, 1996
)
. The extra bias rate,
O\left(\frac{b^{4}}{h}\right)
or
O\left(\frac{\max\{h,b\}^{4}}{h}\right)
, arises from a higher-order term in Taylor’s expansion and becomes asymptotically negligible when
h\gtrsim b
as
n\to\infty
; see (
29
) and (
31
) in
Section B.2
for example. The stochastic variation term
\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})-\mathbb{E}\left[\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})\right]
can be approximated (up to a scaled factor
\sqrt{nh^{3}b^{d}}
) by an empirical process, leading to the upper bound of its uniform rate of convergence as in
Einmahl and Mason (2005)
. Notably, when using the (partial) local quadratic regression (
q=2
) to obtain
\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})
, the bandwidths
h\asymp b
that optimally trades off the bias and variance in Lemma
3
are of order
O\left(n^{-\frac{1}{d+7}}\right)
, resulting in the optimal rate for derivative estimation established in
Stone (1980)
;
Stone (1982)
.

The uniform rate of convergence in Lemma
3
is useful not only for studying the asymptotic behaviors of our integral estimator (
10
) and localized derivative estimator (
14
) in
Theorem 4
but also for analyzing the bootstrap consistency in
Section 4.3
. With Lemma
3
, we now present the uniform consistency results for
\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)
in (
10
) and
\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)
in (
14
).

Theorem 4

(Convergence of
\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)
and
\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)
)
.

Let
q>0
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{T}
be a compact set so that
p_{T}(t)
is uniformly bounded away from 0 within
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. Suppose that Assumptions
A1
,
A2
,
A3
,
A4
,
A5
, and
A6
hold. Then, as
h,b,\hslash,\frac{\max\{b,h\}^{4}}{h}\to 0
, and
\frac{n\max\{h,\hslash\}b^{d}}{|\log(\hslash hb^{d})|},\frac{|\log(h\hslash b^{d})|}{\log\log n},\frac{nh^{3}}{|\log h|}\to\infty
, we know that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\left|\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)-\theta(t)\right|

\displaystyle=O\left(h^{q}+b^{2}+\frac{\max\{b,h\}^{4}}{h}\right)+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{|\log h|}{nh^{3}}}+\hslash^{2}+\sqrt{\frac{|\log\hslash|}{n\hslash}}\right)

and

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\left|\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)-m(t)\right|

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)+O\left(h^{q}+b^{2}+\frac{\max\{b,h\}^{4}}{h}\right)+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{|\log h|}{nh^{3}}}+\hslash^{2}+\sqrt{\frac{|\log\hslash|}{n\hslash}}\right).

The proof of
Theorem 4
is in
Section C.3
. We restrict the uniform consistency results in
Theorem 4
to a compact set
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{T}
to avoid issues with the density decay of
p_{T}(t)
near the boundary of
\mathcal{T}
. If
p_{T}(t)
is uniformly bounded away from 0 in its support
\mathcal{T}
, we can take
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}
.

The (uniform) rate of convergence of
\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)
consists of two parts. The first part
O_{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)
arises from the sample average of
Y_{i},i=1,...,n
and is asymptotically negligible. The second dominant part is due to the integral component:

\widehat{\Delta}_{h,b}(t)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\widetilde{t}=T_{i}}^{\widetilde{t}=t}\widehat{\theta}_{C}(\widetilde{t})d\widetilde{t},

(16)

whose rate of convergence is determined by
\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)
. Furthermore, the (uniform) rate of convergence of
\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)
interweaves the rate
O\left(h^{q}+b^{2}+\frac{\max\{b,h\}^{4}}{h}\right)+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{|\log h|}{nh^{3}}}\right)
for estimating a one-dimensional derivative-like quantity
\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})\big|T=t\right]
with the rate
O(\hslash^{2})+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{|\log\hslash|}{n\hslash}}\right)
for estimating the conditional CDF
\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}(\bm{s}|t)
. Similar rates of convergence for the additive model (
7
) have also been obtained by
Fan et al. (1998)
.

4.3
Validity of the Bootstrap Inference

Before proving the consistency of our bootstrap procedure in
Section 3.3
, we derive the asymptotic linearity of our integral estimator (
10
) and localized derivative estimator (
14
) as intermediate results.

Lemma 5

(Asymptotic linearity)
.

Let
q\geq 2
in the local polynomial regression for estimating
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s})
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{T}
be a compact set so that
p_{T}(t)
is uniformly bounded away from 0 within
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. Suppose that Assumptions
A1
,
A2
,
A3
,
A4
,
A5
, and
A6
hold. Then, if
b\lesssim h\asymp n^{-\frac{1}{\gamma}}
and
\hslash\asymp n^{-\frac{1}{\varpi}}
for some
\gamma,\varpi>0
such that
\frac{nh^{5}}{\log n}\to c_{1}
and
\frac{n\hslash^{5}}{\log n}\to c_{2}
for some finite number
c_{1},c_{2}\geq 0
and
\sqrt{nh^{3}\max\{h,\hslash\}^{4}},\frac{h^{3}\log n}{\hslash},\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{n\hslash}},\frac{\log n}{n\max\{h,\hslash\}b^{d}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, then for any
t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, we have that

\sqrt{nh^{3}}\left[\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)-\theta(t)\right]=\mathbb{G}_{n}\bar{\varphi}_{t}+o_{P}(1)\quad\text{ and }\quad\sqrt{nh^{3}}\left[\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)-m(t)\right]=\mathbb{G}_{n}\varphi_{t}+o_{P}(1),

where
\bar{\varphi}_{t}(Y,T,\bm{S})=\frac{C_{K_{T}}[Y-\mu(T,\bm{S})]}{\sqrt{h}\cdot p_{T}(t)}\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)K_{T}\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)
for some constant
C_{K_{T}}>0
that only depends on the kernel
K_{T}
and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi_{t}\left(Y,T,\bm{S}\right)&=\mathbb{E}_{T_{1}}\left[\int_{T_{1}}^{t}\bar{\varphi}_{\widetilde{t}}(Y,T,\bm{S})\,d\widetilde{t}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{T_{1}}\left\{\int_{T_{1}}^{t}\frac{C_{K_{T}}\left[Y-\mu(T,\bm{S})\right]}{\sqrt{h}\cdot p_{T}(\widetilde{t})}\left(\frac{T-\widetilde{t}}{h}\right)K_{T}\left(\frac{T-\widetilde{t}}{h}\right)d\widetilde{t}\right\}.\end{split}

(17)

Furthermore, we have the following uniform results as:

\displaystyle\left|\sqrt{nh^{3}}\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\left|\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)-\theta(t)\right|-\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}|\mathbb{G}_{n}\bar{\varphi}_{t}|\right|\asymp\left|\sqrt{nh^{3}}\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\left|\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)-m(t)\right|-\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}|\mathbb{G}_{n}\varphi_{t}|\right|

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\sqrt{nh^{3}\max\{h,\hslash\}^{4}}+\sqrt{\frac{h^{3}\log n}{\hslash}}+\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{n\hslash}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{nb^{d}\hslash}}\right).

The proof of Lemma
5
is in
Section C.4
, and we make two remarks to this crucial lemma.

Remark 3

(Non-degeneracy and validity of pointwise confidence intervals)
.

We prove in Lemma
C.1
of
Section C.4
that the variances
\text{Var}\left[\varphi_{t}(Y,T,\bm{S})\right]
and
\text{Var}\left[\bar{\varphi}_{t}(Y,T,\bm{S})\right]
of the influence functions in (
17
) are positive for each
t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, provided that
\mathrm{Var}(Y|T=t,\bm{S}=\bm{s})
is finite and uniformly bounded away from 0 for all
(t,\bm{s})\in\mathcal{E}
. This ensures that the asymptotic linearity results in Lemma
5
are non-degenerate. Similarly, the bootstrap estimates
\widehat{m}_{\theta}^{*}(t)
and
\widehat{\theta}_{C}^{*}(t)
are also asymptotically linear given the observed data
\left\{(Y_{i},T_{i},\bm{S}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
; see the proof of
Theorem 7
in
Section C.6
. This indicates that the pointwise bootstrap confidence intervals for
m(t)
and
\theta(t)
in
Section 3.3
are asymptotically valid; see Lemma 23.3 in
van der Vaart (1998)
and related results in
Arcones and Gine (1992)
;
Tang and Westling (2024)
.

Remark 4

(Bandwidth selection and the curse of dimensionality)
.

To ensure that the remainder terms of the asymptotically linear forms in Lemma
5
are of order
o_{P}(1)
, we can choose the bandwidths
h\asymp\hslash
to be of order
O\left(n^{-\frac{1}{5}}\right)
, consistent with typical bandwidth selection methods for nonparametric regression
(
Wasserman, 2006
;
Schindler, 2011
)
. Thus, we can tune
h,\hslash
using standard bandwidth selectors for estimating the regression function
\mu(t,\bm{s})
and conditional CDF
p(\bm{s}|t)
without any explicit undersmoothing. For the bandwidth parameter
b
related to the confounding variables in the local polynomial regression, it must satisfy
\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{\frac{4}{5d}}\lesssim b\lesssim n^{-\frac{1}{5}}
to maintain fast and dimensionally independent rates of convergence for our integral and derivative estimators (
10
) and (
14
). This constraint implies that the dimension of confounding variables has to be
d\leq 4
and reveals that our nonparametric inference procedures for
m(t)
and
\theta_{C}(t)
suffer from the curse of dimensionality. However, when
d\geq 5
, the consistency and asymptotic properties of our estimators
\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)
and
\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)
remain valid, though their rates of convergence become slower and dimensionally dependent.

Apart from the above asymptotic linearity results, we establish couplings between
\sqrt{nh^{3}}\cdot\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\left|\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)-m(t)\right|
and
\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\left|\mathbb{G}_{n}(\varphi_{t})\right|
(and similarly, for
\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)
) in Lemma
5
. These couplings are crucial for deriving the Gaussian approximations of
\widehat{m}{\theta}(t)
and
\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)
in
Theorem 6
and for establishing their bootstrap consistency. Now, consider two function classes

\mathcal{F}=\left\{(v,x,\bm{z})\mapsto\varphi_{t}(v,x,\bm{z}):t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\right\}\;\text{ and }\;\mathcal{F}_{\theta}=\left\{(v,x,\bm{z})\mapsto\bar{\varphi}_{t}(v,x,\bm{z}):t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\right\}

(18)

with
\varphi_{t},\bar{\varphi}_{t}
defined in (
17
) respectively. Let
\mathbb{B},\bar{\mathbb{B}}
be two Gaussian processes indexed by
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
respectively with zero means and covariance functions

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}(\mathbb{B}(f_{1}),\mathbb{B}(f_{2}))=\mathbb{E}\left[f_{1}(\bm{U})\cdot f_{2}(\bm{U})\right]\;\text{ and }\;\mathrm{Cov}(\bar{\mathbb{B}}(g_{1}),\bar{\mathbb{B}}(g_{2}))=\mathbb{E}\left[g_{1}(\bm{U})\cdot g_{2}(\bm{U})\right]

with
\bm{U}=(Y,T,\bm{S})
for any
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{F}
and
g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
.

Theorem 6

(Gaussian approximation)
.

Let
q\geq 2
in the local polynomial regression for estimating
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s})
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{T}
be a compact set so that
p_{T}(t)
is uniformly bounded away from 0 within
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. Suppose that Assumptions
A1
,
A2
,
A3
,
A4
,
A5
, and
A6
hold. If
b\lesssim h\asymp n^{-\frac{1}{\gamma}}
and
\hslash\asymp n^{-\frac{1}{\varpi}}
for some
\gamma\geq\varpi>0
such that
\frac{nh^{5}}{\log n}\to c_{1}
and
\frac{n\hslash^{5}}{\log n}\to c_{2}
for some finite number
c_{1},c_{2}\geq 0
and
\frac{\hslash}{h^{3}\log n},\hslash n^{\frac{1}{3}}\log n,\frac{\sqrt{n\hslash}}{\log n},\frac{n\max\{h,\hslash\}b^{d}}{\log n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, then there exist Gaussian processes
\mathbb{B},\bar{\mathbb{B}}
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{u\geq 0}\left|\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\left(\sqrt{nh^{3}}\cdot\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\left|\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)-m(t)\right|\leq u\right)-\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\left(\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}|\mathbb{B}(f)|\leq u\right)\right|

\displaystyle\asymp\sup_{u\geq 0}\left|\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\left(\sqrt{nh^{3}}\cdot\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\left|\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)-\theta(t)\right|\leq u\right)-\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\left(\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{\theta}}|\bar{\mathbb{B}}(g)|\leq u\right)\right|

\displaystyle=O\left(\left(\frac{\log^{5}n}{nh^{3}}\right)^{\frac{1}{8}}+\left(\frac{\log^{2}n}{nb^{d}\hslash}\right)^{\frac{3}{8}}\right),

where
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
are defined in (
18
).

The proof of
Theorem 6
is in
Section C.5
. It demonstrates that the distributions of
\sqrt{nh^{3}}\cdot\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\left|\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)-m(t)\right|
and
\sqrt{nh^{3}}\cdot\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\left|\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)-\theta(t)\right|
can be asymptotically approximated by the suprema of two separate Gaussian processes respectively. To establish the consistency of our bootstrap inference procedure in
Section 3.3
, it remains to show that the bootstrap versions of the differences
\sqrt{nh^{3}}\cdot\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\left|\widehat{m}_{\theta}^{*}(t)-\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)\right|
and
\sqrt{nh^{3}}\cdot\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\left|\widehat{\theta}_{C}^{*}(t)-\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)\right|
, conditional on the observed data
\bm{U}_{n}=\left\{(Y_{i},T_{i},\bm{S}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
, can also be asymptotically approximated by the suprema of the same Gaussian processes, which are summarized in the following theorem.

Theorem 7

(Bootstrap consistency)
.

Let
\mathbb{U}_{n}=\left\{(Y_{i},T_{i},\bm{S}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
be the observed data. Under the same setup of
Theorem 6
, we have that

\displaystyle\sup_{u\geq 0}\left|\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\left(\sqrt{nh^{3}}\cdot\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\left|\widehat{m}_{\theta}^{*}(t)-\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)\right|\leq u\Big|\mathbb{U}_{n}\right)-\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\left(\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}|\mathbb{B}(f)|\leq u\right)\right|

\displaystyle\asymp\sup_{u\geq 0}\left|\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\left(\sqrt{nh^{3}}\cdot\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\left|\widehat{\theta}_{C}^{*}(t)-\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)\right|\leq u\Big|\mathbb{U}_{n}\right)-\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\left(\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{\theta}}|\bar{\mathbb{B}}(g)|\leq u\right)\right|

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\left(\frac{\log^{5}n}{nh^{3}}\right)^{\frac{1}{8}}+\left(\frac{\log^{2}n}{nb^{d}\hslash}\right)^{\frac{3}{8}}\right),

where
\widehat{m}_{\theta}^{*}(t)
and
\widehat{\theta}_{C}^{*}(t)
are the integral estimator (
10
) and localized derivative estimator (
14
) based on a bootstrap sample
\mathbb{U}_{n}^{*}=\left\{\left(Y_{i}^{*},T_{i}^{*},\bm{S}_{i}^{*}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
.

The proof of
Theorem 7
is in
Section C.6
. These results, together with
Theorem 6
, imply the asymptotic validity of bootstrap uniform confidence bands in
Section 3.3
; see Corollary
8
below with its proof in
Section C.7
.

Corollary 8

(Uniform confidence band)
.

Under the setup of
Theorem 7
, we have that

\displaystyle\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\left(\theta(t)\in\left[\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)-\bar{\xi}_{1-\alpha}^{*},\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)+\bar{\xi}_{1-\alpha}^{*}\right]\text{ for all }t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\right)=1-\alpha+O\left(\left(\frac{\log^{5}n}{nh^{3}}\right)^{\frac{1}{8}}+\left(\frac{\log^{2}n}{nb^{d}\hslash}\right)^{\frac{3}{8}}\right),

\displaystyle\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\left(m(t)\in\left[\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)-\xi_{1-\alpha}^{*},\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)+\xi_{1-\alpha}^{*}\right]\text{ for all }t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\right)=1-\alpha+O\left(\left(\frac{\log^{5}n}{nh^{3}}\right)^{\frac{1}{8}}+\left(\frac{\log^{2}n}{nb^{d}\hslash}\right)^{\frac{3}{8}}\right).

5
Experiments

In this section, we evaluate the finite-sample performances of our proposed integral estimator (
10
) and localized derivative estimator (
14
) through simulation studies. Additionally, we apply these estimators to analyze the causal effects of fine particulate matter on the cardiovascular mortality rate in a case study.

5.1
Parameter Setup

In all experiments, we use the Epanechnikov kernel for
K_{T}
and
K_{S}
(with the product kernel technique) in the (partial) local quadratic regression (
12
). To select the default bandwidth parameters
h,b>0
, we implement the rule-of-thumb method outlined in Appendix A of
Yang and Tschernig (1999)
as:

h_{ROT}=C_{h}\left[\frac{\left(\nu_{0}^{(T)}\right)^{2}\widehat{R}\left(T_{(n)}-T_{(1)}\right)}{4\left(\kappa_{2}^{(T)}\right)^{2}\widehat{C}_{\mu}\cdot n}\right]^{\frac{1}{5}},\quad\bm{b}_{ROT}=C_{b}\left[\frac{d\left(\nu_{0,1}^{(S)}\right)^{2d}\widehat{R}\left(\bm{S}_{(n)}-\bm{S}_{(1)}\right)}{4\left(\kappa_{2,1}^{(S)}\right)^{2}\widehat{C}_{\mu}}\right]^{-\frac{1}{d+5}}n^{-\frac{1}{d+1}},

where
\nu_{0}^{(T)},\nu_{0,1}^{(S)},\kappa_{2}^{(T)},\kappa_{2,1}^{(S)}>0
are defined in Assumption
A6
(a),
\widehat{R}=\frac{1}{n-5}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[Y-\widehat{m}(T_{i},\bm{S}_{i})\right]^{2}
, with
\widehat{m}(t,\bm{s})=\widehat{\beta}_{1}(t,\bm{s})
estimated using global (partial) polynomial regression (
12
) with
q=4
and
K_{T}=K_{S}\equiv 1
. The differences
\bm{S}_{(n)}-\bm{S}_{(1)}
are coordinatewise maxima and minima that approximate the integration ranges, and
\widehat{C}_{\mu,T},\widehat{C}_{\mu,\bm{S}}
are the estimated density-weighted curvatures (or second-order partial derivatives) of
\mu
obtained via coordinatewise global fourth-order polynomials averaged over the observed data
\left\{(T_{i},\bm{S}_{i}),i=1,...,n\right\}
; see also Section 4.3 in
García Portugués (2023)
. To account for variability in covariate scales, we use a bandwidth vector
\bm{b}_{ROT}\in[0,\infty)^{d}
rather than a scalar
b>0
. In addition, unless stated otherwise, we set the scaling constants to be
C_{h}=10,C_{b}=15
for all simulations. Finally, for fair comparisons between our proposed estimators and the naive RA estimators
\widehat{m}_{RA}(t)
in (
9
) and
\widehat{\theta}_{RA}(t)
in Remark
1
, we estimate
\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{s})
and
\widehat{\beta}_{2}(t,\bm{s})
using (partial) local quadratic regression (
12
) with the same bandwidth parameters
h,b
.

For the conditional CDF estimator (
13
), we leverage the Gaussian kernel for
\bar{K}_{T}
and set its default bandwidth parameter
\hslash
to the normal reference rule in
Chacón et al. (2011)
;
Chen et al. (2016)
as
\hslash_{NR}=\left(\frac{4}{3n}\right)^{\frac{1}{5}}\widehat{\sigma}_{T}
, where
\widehat{\sigma}_{T}
is the sample standard deviation of
\{T_{i},i=1,...,n\}
. This rule assumes a normal distribution for
p_{T}(t)
and optimizes the asymptotic bias-variance trade-off, which may oversmooth the conditional CDF estimator
(
Sheather, 2004
)
. Nevertheless, since our proposed estimators of
m(t)
and
\theta(t)
are relatively insensitive to the choice of
\hslash
, and the order of
\hslash_{NR}
aligns with our theoretical requirement (see Remark
4
), we adopt
\hslash_{NR}
in the subsequent analysis. For the bootstrap inference, we set a resampling time of
B=1000
and a significance level of
\alpha=0.05
, targeting 95% confidence intervals and uniform bands for inferring the true dose-response curve
m(t)
and its derivative
\theta(t)
.

5.2
Simulation Studies

We consider three different model settings under the additive confounding model (
7
) for our simulation studies. For each model setting, we generate i.i.d. observations
(Y_{i},T_{i},\bm{S}_{i}),i=1,...,n
.

\bullet

Single Confounder Model:
The data-generating model is already described in (
1
).

Figure 2
:
Comparisons between our proposed estimators and the naive RA estimators across various sample sizes under the single confounder model (
1
). Rows present results for estimating
m(t)
and
\theta(t)
respectively, while columns correspond to different values for
n
.

\bullet

Linear Confounding Model:
We consider the following linear effect model with

Y=T+6S_{1}+6S_{2}+\epsilon,\quad T=2S_{1}+S_{2}+E,\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{S}=(S_{1},S_{2})\sim\text{Uniform}[-1,1]^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2},

(19)

where
E\sim\text{Uniform}[-0.5,0.5]
and
\epsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
are two independent variables. The marginal supports of
T
and
\bm{S}
are
\mathcal{T}=[-3.5,3.5]
and
\mathcal{S}=[-1,1]^{2}
respectively, while the support of conditional density
p(t|\bm{s})
for any
\bm{s}\in\mathcal{S}
is much narrower than
\mathcal{T}
.

Figure 3
:
Comparisons between our proposed estimators and the naive RA estimators across various sample sizes under the linear confounding model (
19
). Rows present results for estimating
m(t)
and
\theta(t)
respectively, while columns correspond to different values for
n
.

\bullet

Nonlinear Confounding Model:
We consider the following nonlinear effect model with

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Y=T^{2}+T+10Z+\epsilon,\quad T=\cos\left(\pi Z^{3}\right)+\frac{Z}{4}+E,\quad Z=4S_{1}+S_{2},\\
&\quad\text{ and }\quad\bm{S}=(S_{1},S_{2})\sim\text{Uniform}[-1,1]^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{split}

(20)

where
E\sim\text{Uniform}[-0.1,0.1]
and
\epsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Due to the nonlinear confounding effect
\cos(\pi Z)
and limited treatment variation
E
, the positivity condition (
A0
) is violated on the generated data from (
20
). Notably, the debiased estimator of
m(t)
proposed by
Takatsu and Westling (2022)
(and similarly,
Kennedy et al. 2017
) fails to recover
m(t)
under this data model, because some of their pseudo-outcomes have invalid values caused by near-zero estimated conditional densities of
p(t|\bm{s})
.

Figure 4
:
Comparisons between our proposed estimators and the naive RA estimators across various sample sizes under the linear confounding model (
20
). Rows present results for estimating
m(t)
and
\theta(t)
respectively, while columns correspond to different values for
n
.

In the presence of confounding variables, regressing
Y_{i}
’s to
T_{i}
’s in the generated data from each model only leads to biased estimates of
m(t)
. We apply our proposed integral estimator
\widehat{m}_{\theta}(t)
and localized derivative estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{C}(t)
with nonparametric bootstrap inference in
Section 3.3
to the data generated by each model above with varying sample sizes
n
. We also compare our proposed estimators to the naive RA estimators in (
9
) and Remark
1
. The results are presented in
Figure 2
,
Figure 3
, and
Figure 4
. As sample size increases, the violations of positivity become more pronounced, causing naive RA estimators to exhibit greater instability, especially near the boundary of the marginal support
\mathcal{T}
. Because of the positivity violations, fine-tuning the bandwidth parameters
h,b
does not improve the stability or consistency of the naive RA estimators. In contrast, our proposed estimators approach the true dose-response curves and its derivatives as sample size increases, with narrower pointwise confidence intervals and uniform bands. These results demonstrate the consistency of our proposed estimators under violations of the positivity condition (
A0
).

5.3
Case Study: Effect of PM
2.5
on Cardiovascular Mortality Rate

Air pollution, especially fine particulate matter with diameters less than 2.5
\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro m}
(PM
2.5
), is known to be associated with increased cardiovascular disease risk
(
Brook et al., 2010
;
Krittanawong et al., 2023
)
. Recent studies have also demonstrated a positive association between PM
2.5
levels (
$\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro g}$/m^{3}
) and county-level cardiovascular mortality rates (CMR; deaths/100,000 person-years) in the United States, with socioeconomic factors being well-controlled
(
Wyatt et al., 2020a
)
.

To showcase the applicability of our proposed integral and localized derivative estimators, we apply them to analyzing the PM
2.5
-CMR data from
Wyatt et al. (2020b)
, which contain average annual CMRs (outcome variable
Y
) and PM
2.5
concentrations (exposure variable
T
) across
n=2132
counties from 1990 to 2010. These data were sourced from the US National Center for Health Statistics and Community Multiscale Air Quality modeling system, respectively. The covariate vector
\bm{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{10}
consists of two parts. The first part includes two spatial confounding variables, latitude and longitude of each county, to capture spatial dependence. The second part comprises eight county-level socioeconomic factors from the U.S. census: population in 2000, civilian unemployment rate, median household income, percentage of female households without spouses, percentage of vacant housing units, percentage of owner occupied housing units, percentage of high school graduates or above, and percentage of households in poverty. For each county in a given year, we assign the values of socioeconomic variables based on the nearest U.S. Census year (1990, 2000, or 2010) and compute the averages of
Y,T,\bm{S}
for each county over the 21 years to construct the final dataset. Following
Takatsu and Westling (2022)
, we focus on PM
2.5
levels between 2.5
$\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro g}$/m^{3}
and 11.5
$\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro g}$/m^{3}
to minimize boundary effects.

We apply our proposed integral estimator (
10
) and localized derivative estimator (
14
) to the final data with bandwidth parameters specified in
Section 5.1
. To account for scale differences between treatment
T
and covariates in
\bm{S}
, we multiply the (coordinatewise) standard deviations of
T
and
\bm{S}
on the data to
h_{ROT},\bm{b}_{ROT}
respectively and set
C_{h}=16,C_{b}=23
for smoothing the estimated derivatives. To examine confounding effects, we compare three modeling settings:

(i)

regress
Y
on
T
alone via local quadratic regression estimators;

(ii)

regress
Y
on
T
with spatial locations;

(iii)

regress
Y
on
T
with both spatial and socioeconomic covariates.

Setting (i) ignores confounding effects. Setting (ii) assumes that spatial locations fully account for confounding effects, and our proposed estimators are valid if, for example, no interactions exist between PM
2.5
levels (
T
) and spatial locations (
\bm{S}
), as in model (
7
). Setting (iii) incorporates additional socioeconomic factors as confounding variables, with our estimators remaining valid if interaction effects between PM
2.5
levels (
T
) and all confounding variables (
\bm{S}_{1},\bm{S}_{2}
) are present but can be resolved by conditioning on a subset of key confounding variables (
\bm{S}_{1}
), as in model (
8
).

Results in the left panel of
Figure 5
show that without adjusting for any confounding variables, the fitted curve is non-monotonic, peaking at around 8
$\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro g}$/m^{3}
. After controlling for spatial locations, the relationship flatten but remains non-monotonic when the PM
2.5
level is above 8
$\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro g}$/m^{3}
. Only when we incorporate both spatial and socioeconomic covariates can the estimated curve between PM
2.5
and CMR become monotonically increasing. The estimated changing rate of CMR with respect to PM
2.5
(
i.e.
, estimated derivative curve) in the right panel of
Figure 5
